Unifying cubical and multimodal type theory
Frederik Lerbjerg Aagaard ORCID:0000-0002-9132-0098, Magnus Baunsgaard Kristensen, Daniel Gratzer ORCID:0000-0003-1944-0789, Lars Birkedal ORCID:0000-0003-1320-0098

Abstract
. In this paper we combine the principled approach to modalities from multimodal type theory (MTT) with the computationally well-behaved realization of identity types from cubical type theory (CTT). The result—cubical modal type theory (Cubical MTT)—has the desirable features of both systems. In fact, the whole is more than the sum of its parts: Cubical MTT validates desirable extensionality principles for modalities that MTT only supported through ad hoc means. We investigate the semantics of Cubical MTT and provide an axiomatic approach to producing models of Cubical MTT based on the internal language of topoi and use it to construct presheaf models. Finally, we demonstrate the practicality and utility of this axiomatic approach to models by constructing a model of (cubical) guarded recursion in a cubical version of the topos of trees. We then use this model to justify an axiomatization of Löb induction and thereby use Cubical MTT to smoothly reason about guarded recursion.

1.
Introduction

Type theorists have produced a plethora of variants of type theory since the introduction of
Martin-Löf type theory (
MLTT
), each of which attempts to refine
MLTT
to enhance its
expressivity or convenience. Unfortunately, even extensions of type theory which appear orthogonal
cannot be carelessly combined. Expert attention is frequently necessary to ensure that combinations
of extensions retain desirable properties of base type theory. We are particularly interested in two
families of extensions to
MLTT
:
cubical type theories

[
CCHM18
,
ABC+21
]
and

(Fitch-style) modal type theories

[
Clo18
,
BCM+20
,
GKNB20
]
.

Both of these lines of research aim to increase the expressivity of type theory, but along different
axes. Cubical type theory gives a higher-dimensional interpretation to the identity type and thereby
obtains a more flexible notion of equality along with a computational interpretation of

univalent foundations

[
Uni13
]
. Meanwhile, modal dependent type theory (
MTT
) extends

MLTT
with connectives which need not commute with substitution, allowing for type theory to model
phenomena such as guarded recursion, axiomatic cohesion, or
parametricity
[
BMSS12
,
Shu18
,
ND18
]
.

While combining two complex type theories like this is not a task to be undertaken frivolously,
experience has shown that a principled mixture of these two type theories would be useful. Indeed,
modalities naturally appear in synthetic homotopy theory
[
Shu18
]
but without an
apparatus like
MTT
, these extensions must be handled in an ad-hoc way, which can easily disrupt
the desirable properties of type theory. Moreover, cubical type theory’s version of equality
validates invaluable principles like function extensionality and a cubical variant of
MTT
would
thus eliminate the need to postulate such principles
[
BBC+19
,
KMV21
]
.

Prior to discussing how
MTT
\square
fuses these systems, we set the stage by introducing both
cubical type theory and multimodal type theory separately.

Cubical type theory

Cubical type theory originates from the broader class of
homotopy type theories
whose study
was instigated by Voevodsky’s observation that the intensional identity type could be realized as a
(homotopical) path space
[
KL21
]
. This shift in perspective justifies the inclusion of
the univalence axiom which postulates an equivalence between equalities of elements of a
universe and equivalences of the denoted types. While univalence has many pleasant consequences
(function extensionality, effectivity of quotients, etc.), the addition of such an axiom disrupts
crucial properties of the type theory. In particular, it is not possible to compute in such a
theory. In order to rectify this issue, cubical type theory was introduced and shown to
simultaneously support computation and validate the univalence axiom.

Cubical type theory extends
MLTT
with an interval object
\mathbb{I}
along with a function space—a
path space—to hypothesize over it. Intuitively,
\mathbb{I}
abstracts the interval
[0,1]
and this
connection is enhanced by the addition of operations,
e.g.

0,1:\mathbb{I}
. Accordingly,
\mathbb{I}\to A

classifies lines in
A
and by iterating we obtain squares, cubes, and arbitrary
n
-cubes in
A
.

While homotopy type theory recasts the identity type from
MLTT
as a path in a space, cubical type
theory begins with paths and forces them to behave like an identity type. We therefore isolate a
subtype
\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a_{0},a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a_{0},a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a_{0},a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a_{0},a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
of paths
\mathbb{I}\to A
whose values at
0
and
1
are
a_{0}
and
a_{1}
. By
further equipping
A
with
Kan operations
,
\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a_{0},a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a_{0},a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a_{0},a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a_{0},a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
becomes a new model for the
identity type. The Kan operations are subtle and complex but without them
\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle-,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle-,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is not
even an equivalence relation. The flexibility afforded by these Kan operations, however, allows
cubical type theory to support a computational interpretation of univalence.

Remark 1
.

The path type of a type with Kan operations is not a perfect enhancement of Martin-Löf’s
identity type: the latter satisfies definitional equalities not enjoyed by the former. However,
these two types share the same universal property and so we shall use the term identity type to
refer to both and use
intensional identity type
or
path type
to distinguish them.

MTT
and Fitch-style modal type theories

We now turn from cubical type theory to modal type theory. Like cubical type theory, the motivations
for modalities—type constructors which do not necessarily respect substitution—are semantic;
many models of type theory have further structure which does not directly commute with
substitution but would still be useful to internalize. For instance, the global sections comonad
of a presheaf model is frequently essential for working internally to the
model
[
CBGB15
,
LOPS18
,
Shu18
]
, but it almost never commutes with substitution.

Unfortunately, much of the convenience of
MLTT
hinges on the fact that all operators do commute
with substitution, so introducing a modality tends to disrupt nearly every property of
importance. In order to cope with this contradiction, modal type theories like

MTT

[
GKNB20
]
have carefully isolated classes of modalities which can be safely
incorporated into type theory while preserving properties such as canonicity and
normalization
[
Gra22
]
. In fact,
MTT
can be instantiated with an
arbitrary collection of (weak) dependent right adjoints
[
BCM+20
]
. The metatheory of

MTT
applies irrespective of the choice of mode theory, and therefore
MTT
can be seen as a

general
modal type theory, suitable for instantiation with a wide variety of different
modalities to specialize the type theory to capture particular models.

In practice,
MTT
is parameterized by a mode theory, a 2-category used to describe the modalities
in play. The objects of the mode theory correspond to individual copies of
MLTT
linked together
by the modalities, the morphisms of the mode theory. The 2-cells of the mode theory induce natural
transformations between modalities. For instance, in order to model the global sections comonad we
pick a mode theory with one mode
m
, one modality
\mu
, and a collection of 2-cells shaping this
modality into a comonad,
e.g.
, 2-cells
\mu
\mu\circ\mu
and
\mu
\mathsf{id}_{m}

subject
to several equations. Upon instantiating
MTT
with this mode theory we obtain a type theory with a
comonad already known to satisfy many important metatheorems.

Towards Cubical
MTT

In
[
GKNB20
]
, each mode of
MTT
contains a copy of
MLTT
. Unfortunately, the type
theory therefore inherits the well-known limitations of
MLTT
: the intensional identity type is difficult to
work with, function extensionality is not satisfied. One can resolve these issues by adding equality
reflection to
MTT
, but this disrupts the decidability of type-checking. Moreover, several
modalities arise in the context of homotopy type theory
[
Shu18
]
and adapting
MTT
to
these models requires simply postulating univalence, thereby conferring the same set of
difficulties.

We introduce
MTT
\square
, a unification of Cubical type theory and
MTT
. To a first approximation,

MTT
\square
replays the theory of
MTT
, after replacing
MLTT
with cubical type theory. One
thereby obtains a modal type theory with different modes—now copies of cubical type
theory—connected by arbitrary dependent right adjoints. Moreover,
each mode now satisfies univalence and function extensionality.

Beyond this, a computation rule for Kan operations in modal types is needed for computation (and thus for normalization), but it is not immediately well-typed.
Indeed, a key challenge in combining
MTT
with
CTT
is exactly to capture sufficient interactions between modal and cubical aspects for this rule to be well-typed, whilst not making greater demands than the intended models can bear.

The switch from using
MLTT
to using
CTT
in
MTT
also improves modal types. For instance, in

[
Gra22
]
special care is taken to include
crisp induction
in order
to validate the modal counterpart to function extensionality. While this addition preserves
normalization and canonicity, modal extensionality is independent of
MTT
. In
MTT
\square
, by
contrast, the corresponding principle is simply provable (Theorem
5
).

We show that models of
MTT
\square
can be assembled from certain models of cubical type theory
connected by right adjoints. In particular, given coherent functors
f_{\mu}:\hbox to44.4pt{\vbox to8.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.33278pt\lower-1.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

there is a model of
MTT
\square
which realizes context categories as
\mathbf{PSh}_{\mathbf{cSet}}(\mathcal{C}_{m})
and
modalities as right Kan extension
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle f_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle f_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{)}}_{*}
. This ensures, for example, that despite the
complexity of both
MTT
and cubical type theory, it is easily shown that
MTT
\square
,
appropriately instantiated, models cubical guarded recursion
[
BBC+19
,
KMV21
]
.
Indeed, we show that the cubical underpinnings of
MTT
\square
improve the presentation of guarded
recursion in
MTT

[
GKNB21
]
.

The development of this theory of models also implies the soundness of
MTT
\square
. We further
conjecture, but do not prove, that the normalization results of
[
Gra22
]

for
MTT
and
[
SA21
]
for cubical type theory can be appropriately combined into a
normalization proof for
MTT
\square
.

Contributions

We contribute
MTT
\square
, a synthesis of cubical type theory and
MTT
, and a foundation for
multimodal and higher-dimensional type theories. In
section 2
we recapitulate
the
basics of cubical type theory and
MTT
and in
section 3

we present the definition of
MTT
\square
and further prove the aforementioned modal extensionality
principle. Finally, in
section 4
we introduce the model theory of
MTT
\square
and
further show that cubical presheaves and certain essential geometric morphisms assemble into models.
We then apply this theory to cubical guarded recursion in
section 5
and explore the
improved presentation of guarded recursion.

2.
Cubical and multimodal type theory

We now recall the essential details of cubical type theory
[
CCHM18
]
and

MTT

[
GKNB21
]
. We focus mostly on the portions relevant for
MTT
\square
and
refer the reader to the existing literature for a more thorough introduction. Readers familiar with
both systems may safely proceed to
section 3
.

2.1.
Cubical type theory

CTT
begins by extending
MLTT
with a primitive interval
\mathbb{I}
and algebraic structure to mimic
the real interval
[0,1]
. Terms of type
A
which assume
dimension variables

i,j,k:\mathbb{I}
correspond to
n
-cubes (lines, squares, cubes) in
A
. Concretely, we add a new
context formation rule
\Gamma,i:\mathbb{I}
and a new syntactic class of
dimension terms

\Gamma\vdash r:\mathbb{I}
:

(Abstract interval)

r,s:\mathbb{I}

\Coloneqq

i{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\;\mid\;}0{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\;\mid\;}{}1{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\;\mid\;}{}1-r{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\;\mid\;}{}r\lor s{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\;\mid\;}{}r\land s

We further identify dimension terms by the equations of De Morgan algebras.

Remark 2
.

We note that there are in fact many variations on cubical type theory which primarily impose
differing amounts of structure on the interval. For our work, we have opted De Morgan cubical type
theory
[
CCHM18
]
, but we expect our work could be adapted to e.g., cartesian cubical type
theory
[
ABC+21
]
.

Next, we add
path types
: a dependent product over the interval. The rules for this new
connective are given in
fig. 1
and—just as with dependent products—path types
enjoy
\beta
and
\eta
rules stating
e.g.

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda i.\ p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\lambda i.\ p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\lambda i.\ p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda i.\ p$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}=p\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle r/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle r/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle r/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle r/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}
. In
addition to
\beta
and
\eta
, paths are equipped with further equalities reducing them at
endpoints,
e.g.
, given
p:\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}
then
p\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}=a:A
.

Remark 3
.

Given
x,y:A
, we write
x\equiv y
when there exists an element of
\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Out of the box, paths define a relation on types which is reflexive and symmetric and which
validates extensionality principles such as function extensionality. They are not, however,
transitive and it is this deficiency that leads to the
Kan composition operation
which forms
the
backbone of
CTT
. Intuitively, this composition operation lets us complete an open
box (an
n
-cube missing certain faces) to an
n
-cube. In order to formalize this geometric
intuition we add the face lattice
\mathbb{F}
, a class of propositions, which we use to codify the open
boxes to be filled. We therefore add another syntactic class
\Gamma\vdash\phi:\mathbb{F}
:

(Face lattice)

\phi,\psi:\mathbb{F}

\Coloneqq

\bot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\;\mid\;}{}\top{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\;\mid\;}{}(r=0){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\;\mid\;}{}(r=1){\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\;\mid\;}{}\phi\lor\psi{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\;\mid\;}{}\phi\land\psi

Elements of
\mathbb{F}
are identified by the equations of distributive lattices as well as the additional
equation
(r=0)\land(r=1)=\bot
.

Elements
\phi:\mathbb{F}
are used to
restrict
a context by assuming
them, denoted
\Gamma,\phi
. This allows us to locally force
\phi
to hold so that,
e.g.
,

i:\mathbb{I},(i=0)\vdash i=0:\mathbb{I}

1
1

1

Note that
(i=0)
on the left-hand side is a
face restriction, whilst
i=0
on the right-hand side is a definitional equality.
.
We can take advantage of an assumption
\phi
in our context through
systems
. The rules for
systems are given in
fig. 1
; intuitively they state that to construct an element in

\Gamma,\bigvee_{i}\phi_{i}\vdash u:A
, it suffices to construct elements

\Gamma,\phi_{i}\vdash u_{i}:A
that agree on the overlap. An element constructed through
this amalgamation restricts appropriately
e.g.
,
\Gamma,\phi_{i}\vdash u=u_{i}:A
.

We are frequently concerned with the behavior of a term after some assumption
\phi
—its

boundary
—and therefore introduce notation for it. We write

\Gamma\vdash a:A\,[\phi\mapsto u]
as shorthand for (1)
a
being a term of
A
and
(2) under the assumption
\phi
,
a=u:A
. With this machinery, we can now formulate the Kan
composition rule, shown in
fig. 1
. This one principle is sufficient to prove the
properties we expect of identity types, including J (path induction).
2
2

2

Unlike in
MLTT
,
however, path induction reduces on reflexivity only up to a path.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash a:A\quad\Gamma\vdash b:A}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\frac{\Gamma,i:\mathbb{I}\vdash p:A}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda i.\ p:\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle p(0),p(1)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle p(0),p(1)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle p(0),p(1)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle p(0),p(1)$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash r:\mathbb{I}\quad\Gamma\vdash p:\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}}{\Gamma\vdash p\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}:A}\frac{\Gamma,\phi_{i}\vdash a_{i}:A\quad\Gamma,\phi_{0}\land\phi_{1}\vdash a_{0}=a_{1}:A}{\Gamma,\phi_{0}\lor\phi_{1}\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\;\phi_{0}\;a_{0},\phi_{1}\;a_{1}\;$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\;\phi_{0}\;a_{0},\phi_{1}\;a_{1}\;$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\;\phi_{0}\;a_{0},\phi_{1}\;a_{1}\;$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\;\phi_{0}\;a_{0},\phi_{1}\;a_{1}\;$}\mathclose{}{\}}}:A}\frac{}{\Gamma,\bot\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\;\;$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\;\;$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\;\;$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\;\;$}\mathclose{}{\}}}:A}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\phi:\mathbb{F}\quad\Gamma,i:\mathbb{I}\vdash A\quad\Gamma,\phi,i:\mathbb{I}\vdash u:A\quad\Gamma\vdash u_{0}:A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}\,[\phi\mapsto u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}]}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{comp}^{i}_{A}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\phi\mapsto u$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\phi\mapsto u$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phi\mapsto u$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phi\mapsto u$}\mathclose{}{]}}\,u_{0}:A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle 1/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle 1/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}\,[\phi\mapsto u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle 1/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle 1/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}]}

Figure 1.
Selected rules from
CTT

We review the proof that path equality is transitive to give the reader a sense of the rule.
Let
A
be a type that does not depend on any interval variables, and suppose
a,b,c:A
,

p:\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, and
q:\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle b,c$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle b,c$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle b,c$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle b,c$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. We form three lines in
A
: The paths
p
and
q
as
well as the constant
a
line. Using these we form a system depending on
i
and
j
given by

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\;(i=0)\;a,(i=1)\;q\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}\;$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\;(i=0)\;a,(i=1)\;q\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}\;$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\;(i=0)\;a,(i=1)\;q\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}\;$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\;(i=0)\;a,(i=1)\;q\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}\;$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
. The path
p
forms an extension of this system at

j=0
, and so we can form the path

\lambda i.\ \mathsf{comp}^{j}_{A}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle(i=0)\mapsto a,(i=1)\mapsto q\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle(i=0)\mapsto a,(i=1)\mapsto q\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle(i=0)\mapsto a,(i=1)\mapsto q\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle(i=0)\mapsto a,(i=1)\mapsto q\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}\,p\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, which will reduce to the
j=1
part of our designed system, i.e.,
a
at
i=0
and

q\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}=c
at
i=1
, thus proving transitivity. We think of the input data as an open
box with bottom
p
and sides given by the system; in this analogy the composition forms a lid
completing the outer square.

a
a
b
c
a
p\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
q\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}\mathclose{}{)}}

2.2.
Multimodal type theory

Figure 2.
Selected rules from
MTT

To a first approximation,
MTT
is a collection of copies of
MLTT
for each
m:\mathcal{M}
,
connected by dependent adjunctions
[
BCM+20
]
.
MTT
is parameterized by a mode
theory
\mathcal{M}

[
LS16
]
, a strict 2-category. Each object
m,n,o:\mathcal{M}
is assigned
to a distinct
mode
: a copy of
MLTT
complete with its own judgments (
\Gamma\ \mathsf{cx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
,

\Gamma\vdash M:A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
, …). Many of the rules of
MTT
(dependent sums, inductive types, etc.) are

mode-local
and taken as-is from
MLTT
; the interesting features of
MTT

arise from allowing modes to interact with each other.

Modes are connected to each other by
modalities
: a morphism
\mu :\hbox to31.7pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.00117pt\lower-0.09998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

induces a modality sending types
A
from mode
n
to types
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
in mode
m
. The actual
presentation of modalities is necessarily complex because of dependence: given a type
\Gamma\vdash A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}{n}}
,
there is no obvious choice of context in mode
m
for
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
.
MTT
resolves this tension in
Fitch-style
[
Clo18
]
and pairs each modality with an adjoint action on contexts. In
particular, given a modality
\mu :\hbox to31.7pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.00117pt\lower-0.09998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

, we can obtain a new context

\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\ \mathsf{cx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}

from
\Gamma\ \mathsf{cx}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
. Further rules turn
-,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
into a functor between
categories of contexts and substitutions at modes
n
and
m
; intuitively a left
adjoint to the modal type former
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
. See the introduction and formation rules for

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
recorded in
fig. 2
.

The elimination rule for
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is complex because we cannot ‘reverse’ the introduction rules
without violating the admissibility of substitution. Instead,
MTT
annotates each variable
in the context and replaces
\Gamma,x:A
with
\Gamma,x:(\mu\mid A)
. One can access a variable
annotated with
\mu
if and only if it appears behind precisely
-,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
. The elimination
rule uses these annotations to implement a
modal induction principle
and allows one to
reduce the
process of constructing an element of
B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle a/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle a/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}
for some

\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash a:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
to the case
B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(y)/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(y)/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(y)/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(y)/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}
for some fresh

y:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu\circ\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu\circ\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu\circ\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu\circ\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
; see the precise rule in
fig. 2
.

Thus far we have not mentioned the (2-)categorical structure of
\mathcal{M}
, but it is this additional
structure which allows us to control the behavior of modalities. In fact, the operation sending a
modality
\mu
to
-,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
is 2-functorial so that,
e.g.
,

\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}},\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}=\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\circ\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\circ\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\circ\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\circ\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
. This fact is reflected into
types; the assignment
\mu\mapsto\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
is essentially pseudo-functorial.
Consequently, a 2-cell

\alpha :\hbox to27.88pt{\vbox to7.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.01274pt\lower-1.94443pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

in
\mathcal{M}
induces a substitution

\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\}}}_{\Gamma}:\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
which in turn produces a
collection
of functions
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
. By modifying the equalities and 2-cells of

\mathcal{M}
, we can force
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
to become,
e.g.
, a comonad, a right adjoint, etc.

MTT
also extends dependent products to hypothesize over types annotated with modalities
other than
\mathsf{id}
, i.e., to abstract over

x:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{)}}

[
GKNB21
]
. While these
modal dependent
products
are convenient, we refrain from discussing them here for simplicity.

3.
MTT
\square

Cubical multimodal type theory (
MTT
\square
) enhances
MTT
with a better behaved identity type
and univalence by combining it with
CTT
. Like
MTT
,
MTT
\square
is parameterised by a mode
theory, i.e., a 2-category of modes, modalities, and 2-cells. Whereas
MTT
has a copy of
MLTT

at each mode,
MTT
\square
has a copy of
CTT
. A guiding principle in the design of
MTT
\square

is that cubical and modal aspects should be
orthogonal
to each other. In particular, the
primitives of each system should interact as little as possible with primitives from the other. To
realize this, we add certain exchange principles in
subsection 3.1
which are then
applied in
subsection 3.2
to define composition for modal types.

We detail the novel aspects of
MTT
\square
and refer to
Appendix A
for an exhaustive
account.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash r:\mathbb{I}_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}{\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash r^{\mu}:\mathbb{I}_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}}\quad\textsc{lab={int/exc}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash\phi:\mathbb{F}_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}}{\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash\phi^{\mu}:\mathbb{F}_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}}\quad\textsc{lab={face/exc}}\\

3.1.
Cubical exchange

The orthogonality principle of
MTT
\square
dictates that the interval should be minimally
impacted by the action of a modality on the context. Accordingly, we add
exchange
operations
. Concretely, given a dimension term
\Gamma\vdash r:\mathbb{I}_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
, we add a new dimension term

\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash r^{\mu}:\mathbb{I}_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}
, see
3
in

fig. 3
. We demand that this operation is a morphism of De Morgan algebras and
is lax natural, e.g.
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle r\land s$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle r\land s$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle r\land s$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle r\land s$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mu}=r^{\mu}\land s^{\mu}
and

r^{\mu\circ\nu}=\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle r^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle r^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle r^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle r^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\nu}
. Using this operation, it is
possible to derive the
exchange substitution

\Gamma,i:\mathbb{I}_{m},\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash\sigma_{\mu}:\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}},i:\mathbb{I}_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
, see
3
.
Finally, we add an inverse to this, see
3
, making
\Gamma,i:\mathbb{I}_{m},\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}

isomorphic to
\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}},i:\mathbb{I}_{n}
, once again in accordance with the orthogonality
principle.

The case is entirely symmetrical for elements of the face lattice and the corresponding restricted
contexts. Concretely, given a face
\Gamma\vdash\phi:\mathbb{F}_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
, we add a new face

\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash\phi^{\mu}:\mathbb{F}_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}
, see
3
. Similarly to before,
we require this operation to be a morphism of bounded lattices and be lax natural, but we further
require that it matches with the corresponding operation on the interval via the equation

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle r=0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle r=0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle r=0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle r=0$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mu}=\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle r^{\mu}=0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle r^{\mu}=0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle r^{\mu}=0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle r^{\mu}=0$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Thus,
-^{\mu}
commutes with everything but
dimension variables, meaning that
\phi^{\mu}
is precisely
-^{\mu}
applied to every
dimension variable in
\phi
. Just as before, we can derive a substitution, to which we add an
inverse, making
\Gamma,\phi,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
and
\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}},\phi^{\mu}

isomorphic, see
3
and
3
.

As we will see shortly, these rules are sufficient for composition in modal types, but one may
still wonder if there would be merit in the addition of inverses to
r^{\mu}
and

\phi^{\mu}
; after all, this would be in line with our orthogonality principle. It turns out,
however, that such an addition would lead to a significant restriction of what models are valid, in
particular, it would invalidate our model of guarded recursion in
section 5
, and we thus
refrain from making such an addition.

As mentioned, the exchange operations respect the 2-categorical structure of the mode theory, and
since the exchange substitutions are derived from the simpler exchange operations, they inherit this
property. We now record one such coherence explicitly for future use.
MTT
\square
inherits a

weakening substitution
from
CTT
:

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{

. One may show
that the two canonical substitutions

\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}},\phi^{\mu}
\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
are equal. Explicitly, we
equate the direct restriction substitution
\uparrow^{\phi^{\mu}}
to
\uparrow^{\phi}.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\circ\tau^{\mu}
.

3.2.
Composition in modal types

Now we can tackle the problem of composition in
MTT
\square
. Composition is, as the other cubical
rules, added to the system locally and satisfies the same computation rules familiar from
CTT

for standard type formers. Modal types will support a computation principle similar to that of
inductive types, allowing us to commute
\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(-)
with
\mathsf{comp}
. Thus the status
of composition in modal types is similar to that of natural numbers, where composition is a formal
operation that reduces on canonical forms, as opposed to
e.g.
dependent sums.

The desired ‘reduction’ is
3
. We take a moment to show that the conclusion
of this rule is well-typed and that the result has the expected boundary.

Inspecting the assumptions of this rule, we note that all but one are equivalent to a
composition problem in
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
where the input terms are of form
\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(u)
and

\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(u_{0})
—with the exception that the assumption
u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}=u_{0}
is slightly stronger
than
necessary—so it is clear that the left-hand side of this equality is well-typed. That the
right-hand side,

\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{comp}^{i}_{A}\,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\phi^{\mu}\mapsto u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\phi^{\mu}\mapsto u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phi^{\mu}\mapsto u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phi^{\mu}\mapsto u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\sigma_{\mu}\circ\tau_{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}\,u_{0})
, is
well-typed is more subtle. Inspecting the rule for composition in
fig. 1
, we see
that we must first verify the following:

(1)

\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash\phi^{\mu}:\mathbb{F}_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}

(2)

\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}},\phi^{\mu},i:\mathbb{I}_{m}\vdash u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\sigma^{\mu}\circ\tau^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\sigma^{\mu}\circ\tau^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sigma^{\mu}\circ\tau^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\sigma^{\mu}\circ\tau^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}:A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\sigma^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\sigma^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sigma^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\sigma^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}

(3)

\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash u_{0}:A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}

(4)

\Gamma,\phi,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash u\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}=u_{0}:A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0/i$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}

All of these are immediate results of the premises of
3
. In particular,

item 4
is precisely the aforementioned stronger premise.

under

Remark 4
.

The composition rule for modal
\Pi
-types is the same as for non-modal
\Pi
-types up to
application of
-,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
and use of exchange substitutions to make it well-typed.

3.3.
Extensionality principles in
MTT
\square

Function extensionality in
MTT
\square
follows directly from function extensionality in
CTT
,
since the rules used in
CTT
are all available mode-locally in
MTT
\square
.
We will prove
modal extensionality
, which cannot be proven in
MTT
:

Theorem 5
.

Given
\mu:n\to m
and
\Gamma\vdash a,b:A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}
, where
A
is
classified by the universe, there is an equivalence
\mathsf{modext}_{a,b}:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\simeq\mathsf{Path}_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a),\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(b)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a),\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(b)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a),\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(b)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a),\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(b)$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Proof 3.1
.

We define the map
\mathsf{modext}_{a,b}
and show it to be an equivalence by constructing an inverse.
Fix
\Gamma\vdash m:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
and
\Gamma\vdash r:\mathbb{I}_{m}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
. We wish to construct

\mathsf{modext}_{a,b}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.
By modal induction it suffices to consider the case where

m=\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(p)
for some
\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash p:\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}
.
Because
p
lives in a locked context whereas
r
does not, we need an exchange operation.
We form
\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash r^{\mu}:\mathbb{I}_{n}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}
, and define

\mathsf{modext}_{a,b}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(p\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle r^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle r^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle r^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle r^{\mu}$}\mathclose{}{)}})
. Towards verifying
that we obtain an inverse using path induction, note that for
\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash c:A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}
we have that

\mathsf{modext}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{refl}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{refl}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{refl}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{refl}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(c)).

Next we define a map
\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{a,b}
in the inverse direction.
4
4

4

We will only need path
induction and modal induction rather than path abstraction to define
\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{a,b}
, meaning
that it can also be defined in
MTT
.
By based path induction along with careful modal
induction, it suffices to define only
\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{a,a}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a))$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. In this case we
define
\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{a,a}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a))$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{refl}(a))
.
For later use, we calculate for
\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash c:A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}
that

\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}\equiv\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{refl}(c)).

We obtain only a path rather than a judgmental equality because path induction computes
only up to a higher path in cubical type theory.

Lastly, we prove that these maps form an equivalence. Let

\Gamma\vdash m:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid\mathsf{Path}_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
. We are to find a path between

\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{a,b}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{modext}_{a,b}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{modext}_{a,b}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{modext}_{a,b}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{modext}_{a,b}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
m
. It suffices to do so when

m=\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{refl}(c))
for some
\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}\vdash c:A{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}n}
where we compute:

\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{modext}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{modext}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{modext}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{modext}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}\equiv m.

For the reverse direction, let
\Gamma\vdash p:\mathsf{Path}_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a),\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(b)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a),\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(b)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a),\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(b)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a),\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(b)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{}\mathop{@}m}
. We need a
path between
p
and
\mathsf{modext}_{a,b}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{a,b}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{a,b}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{a,b}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{a,b}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. We again reduce to the case
where
p=\mathsf{refl}(\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(c))
and compute from there:

\mathsf{modext}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{modext}^{-1}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle p$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\equiv\mathsf{modext}_{c,c}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{refl}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{refl}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{refl}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(\mathsf{refl}(c))$}\mathclose{}{)}}=p
.

4.
Semantics of
MTT
\square

Section
3
toured through
MTT
\square
informally, but in fact,
MTT
\square

can be
presented as a particular
generalized algebraic theory

[
Car78
]
. This
automatically gives rise to a category of models—a variant of the standard
categories with
families

[
Dyb96
]
—with several desirable properties such as the initiality of
syntax. However,
MTT
\square
is complex and the induced definition of model is nearly intractable
to manipulate, let alone construct.

We fracture the definition of model into more manageable pieces, making heavy use of the natural
models of
MTT

[
Awo18
,
GKNB21
]
. In order to construct these models, we
introduce
cubical
MTT
cosmoi
. This is a more compact structure supplementing
MTT

cosmoi
[
Gra22
]
with the ingredients necessary to internally construct a model of

CTT

[
OP18
,
LOPS18
]
. In practice, cosmoi are easier to obtain and suffice for the
most important models
e.g.
those in cubical presheaves.

4.1.
Models of
MTT
\square

to the adjoint action
-.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
contexts and the 2-cells
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}

interpret the natural transformations
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
. We now specify the remaining structure on
top of this functor.

Mode-local structure

Each mode
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}
should contain a complete model of
CTT
, and we specify this in the
language of natural models
[
Awo18
]
which provides a concise description of the
connectives of type theory.

As a model of
CTT
,
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}
has an interval object
\mathbf{I}_{m}:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}
. Just as in

CTT
, we require that
\mathbf{I}_{m}
is a De Morgan algebra and that all products
-\times\mathbf{I}_{m}
exist.

Next, we require a pair of presheaves
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m},\mathcal{T}_{m}:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}})
representing
respectively the collection of terms and types in a given context. Moreover, there is a projection
map
\tau_{m}:\hbox to44.89pt{\vbox to8.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.57584pt\lower-1.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.57584pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

which sends a term to its type. This universe is closed
under dependent sums, products, etc. Each mode also contains an interpretation of the face lattice

\mathbb{F}_{m}:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}})
and face restriction which is used to specify the composition
operations. While complex, this piece of the model is unchanged from
CTT
so we relegate further
details to
Appendix B
.

Modal types

Next we turn to the modal aspect of a model: modal context extension and modal types. Both of these
structures are specified exactly as in
MTT

[
GKNB21
]
, with the small caveat
that we require an additional equality for composition operation on modal types.

which we require has an inverse.

4.2.
Cubical
MTT
cosmoi

Even after the repackaging of models detailed in
subsection 4.1
, a model of

MTT
\square
is still a complex object. There are two orthogonal aspects to this complexity: (1)
constructing the models of cubical type theory in each mode and (2) constructing the network of
modalities and their actions on contexts. Fortunately, there already exists a technique to simplify
(1); rather than construct a model of cubical type theory directly,
[
OP18
]
and

[
LOPS18
]
have shown that any topos satisfying a handful of axioms supports a model of
cubical type theory. Moreover, (2) is partially addressed in
[
Gra22
]
by the notion of an

MTT
cosmos which abstracts several of the difficulties of constructing a model of
MTT
. We now
unify these two ideas to define
cubical
MTT
cosmoi
and prove that they induce a model of

MTT
\square
.

MTT
cosmoi

We will first recall the definition of
MTT
cosmoi and prove that they induce models of
MTT
.

{defi}

A cosmos is a pseudofunctor
F:\hbox to44.45pt{\vbox to6.96pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.58334pt\lower-0.09998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

A cosmos abstracts from the basic situation we encountered in
subsection 4.1
: a
2-functor
F
picking out categories of contexts and the actions of modalities between them. In this
case, we were primarily concerned not with the category
F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, but with
presheaves over

F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. After all, it is the category of presheaves which hosts types and terms and where we
formulate structures like context extension. Careful inspection reveals that we only require the
locally Cartesian closed structure of
\mathbf{PSh}(F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}})
when formulating the rest of the structure
of a model, so it is natural to require only that each mode of a cosmos is locally Cartesian
closed. Indeed, on top of this skeleton we can transport more of the structure of a model to cosmoi:

Theorem 7
.

An extensional
MTT
cosmos induces a model of extensional
MTT
with modal context structure,
which is pseudonaturally equivalent to the pseudofunctor of left adjoints induced by
Lemma
6
. If the pseudofunctor of left adjoints is a strict
2-functor, the modal context structure may instead be chosen to be equal to it.

For each mode
m
, we define the universe of types and terms as the Yoneda embedding of the
universe already present in
\widehat{F}_{!}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}
