\widehat{\tau_{m}}=\mathbf{y}(\alpha_{m}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\tau_{m}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\tau_{m}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tau_{m}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tau_{m}$}\mathclose{}{)}})
. Because
\widehat{F}_{!}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is
finitely complete, this is a representable natural transformation. Moreover, since both
\alpha_{m}

and
\mathbf{y}
preserve LCCC structure, this universe is closed under the types in the types cosmos:
dependent right adjoints for each
\mu
, dependent sums, products, booleans, and extensional
identity types etc. Thus by
[
GKNB21
, Theorem
7.1]

\widehat{F}_{!}

models
extensional
MTT
.

Cubical cosmoi

An
MTT
cosmos
F
interprets each mode as an LCCC
F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}
because locally Cartesian closed
structure is sufficient to specify the connectives of
MTT
. Unfortunately, it is not sufficient to
apply the techniques of
[
OP18
]
and
[
LOPS18
]
and internally construct a model of

CTT
. We therefore isolate the notion of
a LOPS topos
,
6
6

6

Named after the authors of

[
LOPS18
]
containing precisely the required structure. We further define cubical cosmoi
as particularly well-behaved networks of LOPS topoi.

{defi}

A LOPS topos is an elementary topos
\mathcal{E}
with a hierarchy of universes, an object of cofibrations

\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{E}}
\Omega
and a tiny interval object
\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{E}}
subject to the
Orton-Pitts axioms.
7
7

7

In fact, we make use of a slight strengthening of axioms presented by
[
OP18
]
in order
to ensure that
\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{E}}
is an internal De Morgan algebra rather than a connection algebra.

{thmC}

[
[
LOPS18
]
]

There exists a model of
CTT
in every LOPS topos.

Extending a context
X:\mathcal{E}
by an interval variable is given by the product:

X\times\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{E}}
. The
structure of this context extension and of dimension terms more generally follows directly from the
universal property of products along with the De Morgan algebra structure on
\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{E}}
; a
dimension term in context
X
is realized as a morphism
X
\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{E}}
. Similarly, an
element of the face lattice in context
X
is interpreted as a
morphism.
X
\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{E}}
. Restricting a context by such a face is given by
pullback:
8
8

8

We have used the familiar set-comprehension notation for restriction by a
face. Because
\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{E}}
is a subobject of
\Omega
, this coincides with the standard
interpretation of this notation in a topos.

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle X$}\mathrel{{|}}\hbox{$\displaystyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle X$}\mathrel{{|}}\hbox{$\textstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle X$}\mathrel{{|}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle X$}\mathrel{{|}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
X
\mathbf{1}
\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{E}}
\top
\phi

Remark 8
.

Whilst
F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{E}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{E}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{E}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{E}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a De Morgan algebra, we make no assumption that it be isomorphic
to
\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
. Doing so would correspond to adding an inverse to
3
, but as
mentioned in
subsection 3.1
, this is not valid in the model of guarded recursion in

section 5
; explicity, the right adjoint “later” does not preserve the
interval.
The case is the same for the object of cofibrations.

,

•

F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a morphism of LOPS topoi for each modality
\mu
,

•

The interval and face lattice maps are pseudonatural.

Theorem 9
.

Any cubical
MTT
cosmos
F
induces a model of
MTT
\square
with modal context structure
pseudonaturally equivalent to the pseudofunctor of left adjoints induced by
Lemma
6
. If the pseudofunctor of left adjoints is a strict
2-functor, the modal context structure may instead be chosen to be equal to it.

Proof 4.2
.

A cubical
MTT
cosmos is in particular an extensional
MTT
cosmos, meaning that all the rules
from extensional
MTT
can be modelled with the strictified 2-functor
\widehat{F}_{!}
by
Theorem
7
. Equivalence preserves being a LOPS topos, and thus

\widehat{F}_{!}(m)\simeq F_{!}(m)=F(m)
is a LOPS topos, implying we can model all the mode-local
rules added from
CTT
(including composition structures for all non-modal types) with
Theorem
4.2
. It thus remains to construct the exchange principles and composition
structures on modal types.

We claim that
\widehat{F}_{!}
(or rather, the pseudofunctor of right adjoints
\widehat{F}

induced by the dual of Lemma
6
) also has the cubical components
of being a cubical
MTT
cosmoi. We have already argued that
\widehat{F}(m)
is a LOPS
topos since it is equivalent to
F(m)
, the fact the naturality squares of these equivalences
commute up to natural isomorphism is enough to show that
\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a morphism of
LOPS topoi, and the pseudonatural coherence of the isomorphisms is preserved since the
equivalences cohere pseudonaturally.

As a consequence of this, we have at each mode
m:\mathcal{M}
a De Morgan algebra and a bounded
distributive lattice
\mathbf{I}_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}},\mathbf{F}_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}}:\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and for each modality

\land

The right rectangle commutes since the
\alpha_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}}
preserves
\land
, and the
left triangle commutes by the uniqueness of morphisms to products. Preservation of the other
connectives follow similarly.

The final thing to verify for intervals is that the uniquely determined dashed arrow in the
following diagram has an inverse:

\widehat{F}_{!}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\times\mathbf{I}_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\Gamma\times\mathbf{I}_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Gamma\times\mathbf{I}_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma\times\mathbf{I}_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\widehat{F}_{!}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbf{I}_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{I}_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{I}_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\mathbf{I}_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle n$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle n$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle n$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle n$}\mathclose{}{)}}}
\widehat{F}_{!}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\Gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\widehat{F}_{!}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\pi_{2}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\pi_{2}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\pi_{2}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{2}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\alpha_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}}
\widehat{F}_{!}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\pi_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\pi_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\pi_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\pi_{1}
\pi_{2}

This follows from
\alpha_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}}
being invertible and
\widehat{F}_{!}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}

preserving finite limits.

Replaying these arguments for the face lattices completes the construction of the exchange
principles. In particular, while we have not required it,
\beta_{\widehat{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}}
is always
homomorphism of distributive lattices, essentially because
\widehat{F}_{!}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
preserves
monomorphisms and commutes with finite limits and colimits. To construct the interpretations of

\tau_{\mu}
and
\tau^{\mu}
, we observe that it is a (necessarily unique) map witnessing the
equivalence of a pair of subobjects over the interpretation of
\Gamma,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
.

Lastly, we will construct the compositions structures on modal types. For this, we note that the
model of extensional
MTT
obtained from Theorem
7
supports an inverse
operation to
\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(-)
such that every element of a modal type is of the form
\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}(a)
.
Therefore, the equation
3
can be taken as-is to fully define a
composition structure.

4.3.
Cubical presheaves

The intended model of cubical type theory is a variant on the standard presheaf mode with types
interpreted as a variant of Kan cubical sets
[
CCHM18
]
—particular presheaves on the cube
category
\square
realized as the Lawvere theory of De Morgan algebras. One immediate benefit of the
internal construction of a model of
CTT
is to generalize this result from cubical sets to

presheaves valued in cubical sets

[
OP18
]
. Meanwhile, networks of presheaf
categories connected by the essential geometric morphisms induced by functors between base
categories are known to induce models of
MTT

[
GKNB21
, Section 8]
. In fact, a
consequence of Theorem
9
is that these two results can be essentially
combined, thereby giving rise to the most important models of
MTT
\square
.

Proposition 10
.

\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be small categories, let

(1)

The presheaf categories
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C}\times\square)
and
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{D}\times\square)
are LOPS
topoi.

(2)

The adjunction
F^{*}\dashv F_{*}
induces a morphism of LOPS topoi.

(3)

If
F_{!}
is lex the adjunction
F_{!}\dashv F^{*}
induces a morphism of LOPS topoi.

Before proving the above proposition we recall some standard lemmas.

is flat if and only if

is filtered for each

. A proof of this standard fact is given by

[
Bor94
, Proposition 6.1.2]
. We note that this implies in particular that

(F\downarrow d)
is connected.

We can now prove Proposition
10
:

Proof 4.3
.

Note first that
\mathbf{PSh}(\mathcal{C}\times\square)=\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbf{Set}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbf{Set}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbf{Set}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}\times\square$}\mathclose{}{)}}}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbf{Set}$}\mathclose{}{]}}\cong\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbf{cSet}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbf{cSet}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbf{cSet}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbf{cSet}$}\mathclose{}{]}}=\mathbf{PSh}_{\mathbf{cSet}}(\mathcal{C})
. Letting
\mathbb{I},\mathbb{F}:\mathbf{cSet}
be the interval
respectively face lattice from
[
CCHM18
, Section 8.1]
, we define
\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{C}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathbb{I}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{C}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathbb{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}
for
c:\mathcal{C}
and

I:\square
.

For (1) these topoi satisfy the Orton-Pitts axioms as noted in
[
CRS21
]
. To see that
the intervals defined above are tiny we proceed as follows: Using the Yoneda lemma along with the
fact that
\mathbb{I}
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle-,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle-,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-,\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}_{\square}
shows that
\mathbf{y}(c,I)\times\mathbf{I}\cong\mathbf{y}(c,I+\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}\mathclose{}{\}}})
, and we thus calculate:

\displaystyle X^{\mathbf{I}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbf{y}(c,I),X^{\mathbf{I}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbf{y}(c,I),X^{\mathbf{I}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{y}(c,I),X^{\mathbf{I}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{y}(c,I),X^{\mathbf{I}}$}\mathclose{}{]}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbf{y}(c,I)\times\mathbf{I},X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbf{y}(c,I)\times\mathbf{I},X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{y}(c,I)\times\mathbf{I},X$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{y}(c,I)\times\mathbf{I},X$}\mathclose{}{]}}

, and thus exponentiation by
\mathbf{I}
is (naturally isomorphic to)

. As this functor
has a right adjoint, we have shown that
\mathbf{I}
is tiny.

We write
\pi_{\mathcal{C}},\pi_{\mathcal{D}}
for the projections
\mathcal{C}\times\square
\square
and

\mathcal{D}\times\square
\square
respectively. For the second (respectively third) requirement we
show the following:

•

F^{*}
(resp.
F_{!}
) preserves finite limits.

•

\iota:F^{*}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{*}\cong\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}

(resp.
F_{!}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\cong\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{*}
)

•

\iota
is an isomorphism of De Morgan algebras at
\mathbb{I}

(resp. distributive lattices at
\mathbb{F}
).

The remaining conditions of a morphism of LOPS topoi follow automatically from the naturality
of
\alpha
(resp.
\beta
).

For the first item, we note that
F^{*}
preserves all limits since it is a right adjoint, and
that
F_{!}
preserves finite limits by assumption.

Next, the desired isomorphism
F^{*}(\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{*}(X))\cong\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(X)
can be taken to
be the identity, justified by the following computation:

F^{*}(\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{*}(X))(c,I)=\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{*}(X)(F(c),I)=X(I)=\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(X)(c,I)

It is clear that this isomorphism preserves the De Morgan algebra structure when
X=\mathbb{I}
and the
distributive lattice structure when
X=\mathbb{F}
.

It remains to consider these conditions for
F_{!}
. We construct

F_{!}(\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(X))\cong\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{*}(X)
as the composite of a string of natural
isomorphisms:

\displaystyle F_{!}(\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(X))(d,I)

\displaystyle\cong\mathop{\mathsf{colim}}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}:(F\times\mathsf{id}\downarrow(d,I))}\pi_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(X)(c,I^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathsf{colim}}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}:(F\times\mathsf{id}\downarrow(d,I))}X(I^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\mathop{\mathsf{colim}}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle Lemma
12

\displaystyle\cong\mathop{\mathsf{colim}}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}:(F\downarrow d)}X(I)

Lemma
13

\displaystyle\cong X(I)

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{*}(X)(d,I)

In the above calculation, the fourth isomorphism follows by observing that

\mathop{\mathsf{colim}}\nolimits_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}:(F\downarrow d)}X(I)
is the colimit of
a constant diagram over
(F\downarrow d)
; because

F_{!}
is lex, Lemma
11
ensures that
(\mathsf{id}_{\square}\times F\downarrow(d,I))
is
connected, and hence so is
(F\downarrow d)
. The isomorphism then follows from the observation that
colimits of constant, connected diagrams are isomorphic to the value of the diagram.

We must argue that this is an isomorphism of De Morgan algebras when
X=\mathbb{I}
and of distributive
lattices when
X=\mathbb{F}
. Chasing an element through this string of isomorphisms, we send an element
of the colimit
\mathsf{in}_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle to
X\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle g$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle g$}\mathclose{}{)}}(x)
. One can verify that
this preserves the relevant structure when
X
is appropriately specialized. We illustrate the
simple case of interval endpoints: The
0
endpoint of
F_{!}(\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{C}})
at
(d,I)
is given by

\mathsf{in}_{(f_{0},\mathsf{id})}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0:\mathbb{I}(I)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 0:\mathbb{I}(I)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0:\mathbb{I}(I)$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0:\mathbb{I}(I)$}\mathclose{}{)}}
where
f_{0}
is an arbitrary object of the (necessarily
non-empty) category
(F\downarrow d)
. It is clear that this pair is mapped to

0:\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{D}}(d,I)
via the morphisms above.

We can package all of the above results into the following:

Theorem 14
.

Let

be a strict 2-functor, write

for the precomposition, left Kan extension, and right Kan extension

, and write

,

, and

for the induced pseudofunctors.

•

The network of morphisms of LOPS topoi given by the adjunctions

F^{*}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\dashv F_{*}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
induces a model of
MTT
\square
over
\mathcal{M}
with
modal context structure equal to
F^{*}
.

•

The network of morphisms of LOPS topoi given by the adjunctions

F_{{!}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\dashv F^{*}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
induces a model of
MTT
\square
over
{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathsf{coop}}

with modal context structure pseudonaturally equivalent to
F_{{!}}
if each

F_{{!}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is lex.

Proof 4.4
.

By Theorem
9
, it is sufficient to show that
F_{*}
(respectively

F^{*}
) is a cubical
MTT
cosmos. By Proposition
10
, each

\mathbf{PSh}(F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\square)
is a LOPS topos, and each adjunction
F^{*}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\dashv F_{*}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
(respectively
F_{{!}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\dashv F^{*}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
) is a morphism of
LOPS topoi, and we thus need only verify that the interval and face lattice isomorphisms cohere
pseudonaturally.

Consider first the case of the adjunctions
F^{*}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\dashv F_{*}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. In this
case, each interval (respectively face lattice) isomorphism is the identity, and thus since the
left adjoints form a strict 2-functor, the interval objects (respectively face lattice objects)
form a strict 2-natural transformation, which in particular is also a pseudonatural
transformation.

Consider next the case of the adjunctions
F_{{!}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\dashv F^{*}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. To prove
pseudonaturality, we need to prove coherence with identity, composition, and 2-cells. The proofs
are similar, and we illustrate them with the identity case. Since
F_{{!}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a
pseudofunctor, there is a natural isomorphism
F_{{!}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 1_{m}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 1_{m}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1_{m}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1_{m}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\cong\mathsf{id}_{\mathbf{PSh}(F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\square)}
. We must prove that at the interval (respectively face
lattice) object, this is the same isomorphism as the one constructed in
Proposition
10
.

The isomorphism in the proof of Proposition
10
,

F_{{!}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{C}_{m}}^{*}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}},\_$}\mathclose{}{)}}:(F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathsf{id}\downarrow(d,I))}\pi_{F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{)}}}^{*}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle X$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle X$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle X$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle X$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, we get
X\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}\mathclose{}{)}}
as before,
which shows that the two isomorphisms are the same, and thus the identity condition for
pseudonaturality is satisfied.

Remark 15
.

In these results, we have chosen the cofibration classifier in each mode to be

\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{C}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle c,I$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathbb{F}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. While certainly valid, it is also reasonable to define

\mathbf{F}
to simply be the subobject classifier
\Omega
. We leave it to the reader to show that
with this alternative choice of face lattice, Theorem
14
is still
valid.

5.
Proving and programming with guarded recursion

We now turn from theory to practice
9
9

9

Or at least, slightly more practice-adjacent theory!

and consider
guarded
MTT
\square
. We briefly recall guarded recursion. The core idea of
guarded recursion
[
Nak00
]
is to use a modality
\RHD
(pronounced ‘later’) to isolate
recursively produced data to prevent its use until work is done, thereby ensuring productivity. This
modality is equipped with operations making it into an applicative functor
[
MP08
]
which
satisfies
Löb induction
, a powerful guarded fixed-point principle:

allows us to define an element of
A

, the usual problems with fixed-points are avoided.
We consider a variant of guarded recursion which further includes an idempotent comonad

\Box
10
10

10

This is not related to cubes despite what the notation might suggest.
along
with an equivalence
\Box\RHD A\simeq\Box A
.
This last property ensures that guarded type theory
can construct
coinductive
types through Löb induction
[
CBGB15
]
.

To encode guarded recursion in
MTT
\square
, we instantiate the theory with a particular mode
theory and extend it with a pair of
axioms. As a result, we obtain a highly workable guarded type theory supporting the relevant
modalities and operations. Similar work was done for
extensional

MTT
in
[
GKNB21
, Section
9]
; here we show that the improved notion of equality in
MTT
\square
results
in an improved experience.

\square

\widehat{F}
. In particular, because Löb induction holds in the
\mathbf{Set}
-based model,

\widehat{F}
.

This can also be verified by hand as is done in

]
.

5.2.
Programming with guarded
MTT
\square

To see that
MTT
\square
can not merely replicate but also improve on work done in
MTT
, we
now show that Löb induction not only gives a fixpoint but a unique one. In
[
GKNB20
, Theorem
9.5]
this is proven for extensional
MTT
(by introducing equality reflection), but
because of
modal extensionality, we can now prove it with nothing but
MTT
\square
and Löb induction.
Similarly, the results from
[
GKNB21
, Section 9.4]
about guarded and coinductive
streams in guarded
MTT
may also be proven in guarded
MTT
\square
without equality reflection.

6.
Related work

MTT
\square
builds upon two distinct strands of work: cubical and modal type theories. Even though
both lines of research are ongoing, several proposals have already been made which combine
elements of both.

Modal homotopy type theory

Several version of homotopy type theory extended with modalities have been
proposed
[
Shu18
,
RFL21
]
. These type theories aim to increase the expressivity of
HoTT and allow it to better capture some aspects of homotopy theory. Unlike
MTT
\square
, however,
these theories tend to be specialized to various modal situations. They build in the structure of
one specific modality and are hand-crafted to have manageable syntax for that situation. In
contrast,
MTT
\square
follows
MTT
and works for a
class
of modalities, and provides
usable syntax in each case. Moreover, prior type theories in this tradition expand “book
HoTT”
[
Uni13
]
and therefore do not enjoy the good computational properties we conjecture
for

MTT
\square
.

Modal cubical type theory

In order to extend cubical type theory with an
internal
notion of parametricity,
Cavallo
[
Cav21
, Part IV]
has proposed a variant of (cartesian) cubical type theory
extended with connectives and a handful of modalities to capture parametricity. Like
MTT
\square
,
this
cohesive parametric cubical type theory
combines cubical type theory with Fitch-style
modalities. While morally the system is a specialization of
MTT
\square
to a cohesive collection
of modalities, Cavallo takes advantage of several specifics of the intended model to add various
equations to the theory.

Separately, another Fitch-style type theory,
clocked type theory
, has been extended to a
cubical basis
[
KMV21
]
. This theory is used to present guarded recursion, similarly to

section 5
. Unlike guarded
MTT
\square
, clocked cubical type theory includes several
specialized axioms, a more sophisticated collection of guarded modalities, and an account of the
interaction of HITs with parts of the modal machinery.

The extra equations and properties of the modalities in both systems prevent
MTT
\square
from
directly recovering either parametric cubical type theory or clocked cubical type theory. The core
aspects of both, however, are similar to
MTT
\square
and we believe that
MTT
\square
gives a
means of systematically generalizing these calculi to other modal situations.

7.
Conclusions

We contribute
MTT
\square
, a general modal type theory based on cubical type theory and

MTT
. The system can be instantiated to a number of modal situations while still maintaining
computationally effective interpretations of univalence and function extensionality.

While in this work we have introduced the theory and characterized a class of models for it, in the
future we hope to investigate further metatheoretic properties of the system. In particular, both

MTT
and cubical type theory enjoy normalization
[
Gra22
,
SA21
]
, and we
conjecture that these proofs can be combined and generalized to apply to
MTT
\square
. The
introduction of cubical cosmoi takes the first step in this direction: cosmoi are a crucial
ingredient of the proof of normalization for
MTT
. In a separate direction, we hope to investigate
the behavior of more of the mode-local structure of cubical type theory such as
higher
inductive types
and other novelties of cubical type theories.

Appendix A
Rules of
MTT
\square

We here present the official syntax and rules of
MTT
\square
. For the sake of brevity, we omit a
number the rules, especially those lifted from
MTT
or
CTT
; in particular, we omit the
following:

•

The rules for dependent sums, booleans, universes.

•

The equations stating that the interval is a De Morgan algebra.

•

The equations stating that
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mu}
for interval terms is a morphism of De Morgan
algebras.

•

The equations stating that the face lattice is a bounded distributive lattice,

•

The equations stating that
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}^{\mu}
for faces is a morphism of bounded lattices,

•

Miscellaneous equations commuting substitutions past term formers or governing the composition
of substitutions.

Rules that govern the interaction between cubical and modal aspects are marked with a
†
.
At the end there is a section on derived definitions some of which we will use throughout to ease
notation.

Context formation.

{defi}

A modal natural model on a modal context structure consists of

•

for each mode
m:\mathcal{M}
, a presheaf
\mathcal{T}_{m}:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}})
,

•

for each mode
m:\mathcal{M}
, a presheaf
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}})
,

•

for each mode

\mathbb{F}_{m}
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma)

\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}_{m})
\top
\psi
\mathbf{y}(\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\psi})
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}_{m})
\mathbf{y}(\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\phi})
\phi

We can calculate

\displaystyle\psi\circ\mathbf{y}(\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\phi})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{F}_{m}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\phi}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\phi}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\phi}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\phi}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{F}_{m}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\phi}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\phi}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\phi}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\phi}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle=\phi\circ\mathbf{y}(\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\phi})

\displaystyle=\top,

and thus
\psi\circ\mathbf{y}(\uparrow_{m,\Gamma}^{\phi})=\top
, implying the outer square commutates,
and we thus get a canonical morphism
\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}_{m}
\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}_{m}
.

{defi}

A modal face sturcture and a modal natural model (both on the same modal context sturcture) has
systems if

•

for each mode
m:\mathcal{M}
, each context
\Gamma:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}
, any faces
\phi,\psi:\mathbb{F}_{m}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\Gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, each presheaf
X:\mathbf{PSh}(\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}})
, and each commuting diagram

\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\phi\land\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\phi\land\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phi\land\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phi\land\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}_{m})
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}_{m})
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phi$}\mathclose{}{]}}_{m})
\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\phi\lor\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\phi\lor\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phi\lor\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phi\lor\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}_{m})
X

where the arrows in the inner square are the canonical morphisms following from

\phi\land\psi\leq\phi
,
\phi\land\psi\leq\psi
,
\phi\leq\phi\lor\psi
, and

\psi\leq\phi\lor\psi
, if
X
is representable or
\mathbb{F}_{m}
there exists at most one morphism

\mathbf{y}(\Gamma.\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\phi\lor\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\phi\lor\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\phi\lor\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\phi\lor\psi$}\mathclose{}{]}}_{m})
X
such that the diagram commutes, and if
X
is

\mathcal{T}_{m}
or
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}
there exists exactly one such morphism,

{defi}

A path structure on modal interval structure and a modal natural model (both on the same modal
context structure) is a direct translation of the rules for path types, and we will thus not give
the details.

{defi}

A composition structure on modal restriction structure is a direct translation of the rules for
composition, and we will thus not give the details.
